# `web/hosted/` — architecture §6 step 9, the hosted demo's build

The hosted demo is static pages, prerendered from one pinned snapshot of public Devnet state. No code of this project runs on a server when a visitor loads them: the gate fails an output that holds a function, middleware or a rewrite to another host. The decision record is `docs/decisions/ADR-0004-hosted-demo-trust-boundary.md`, `Accepted` at revision 5. The owner ruled its four decisions on 2026-10-19 (`CLAIMS.md` E-331):

- **D-H1, H3.** Prerendered pages. The snapshot is kept indefinitely and rebuilt by hand. The build is isolated from the private stores' files: it runs in containers that mount neither. It is not isolated from the network while it installs and takes the snapshot (below).
- **D-H2.** The risk wording is accepted as written and shown beside every ledger value.
- **D-H3, A.** No issuer figure.
- **D-H4, A.** The output is built and gated locally, then deployed prebuilt from the CLI.

The pages are `web/app/src/hosted/`, a second build target beside the local tier. Four page bodies moved out of the local routes into `web/app/src/views/`, which both tiers render, so those four pages' text exists once. The overview, the issuer page and the banner have hosted texts of their own.

## The release procedure

Each step this repository owns refuses or fails loudly. Step 6 is Vercel's command.

1. **Publish the commit first.** The footer names the commit and links to it in the public repository, and step 5 refuses a commit that is not on the public `main`. Make the public update as `docs/NEXT-SESSION-PROMPT.md` describes, then build from the public clone at that commit.
2. **Build, isolated.** `make hosted-build REPO=<public clone> COMMIT=<sha> URL=https://<name>.vercel.app OUT=<new or empty dir outside the repository>`. The build refuses a directory that is not empty, exports a `git archive` of the commit and refuses it unless its files are exactly the commit's, then runs two containers that see only that tree and each check that neither private store exists: the first installs and takes the snapshot, with network; the second builds and assembles `.vercel/output` with `--network none`. Two canary values must not appear verbatim in the output. `build-manifest.json` is written beside the output, not in it.
3. **Gate the output.** `make check-web-hosted OUT=<dir> COMMIT=<sha>`. This needs the local secret store. Its controls run first, each breaking a copy of the real output, and the gate prints how many passed. On a pass the gate writes `static/gate-verdict.json`, a digest of every other file, which the page's footer shows.
4. **Load it in a browser.** `make hosted-browser-check OUT=<dir>`. A headless Chrome with a fresh profile loads every page under the output's own headers. It fails on a policy violation, a request to anything but the page's origin, an exception or a console error, on any page or after the client-side navigation, a page whose script never ran, a ledger page whose D-H2 wording is missing or not visible (transparent, too small, clipped, or not the element found at the centre of its own text, checked again after the client-side navigation), a URL attribute that the browser resolves to another origin the gate does not admit, or a client-side navigation that loads a new document. Every event belongs to an inspected interval.
5. **Preflight.** `bun run web/hosted/deploy-preflight.ts <dir>` refuses a manifest that does not match the build's schema, including `trial: false` and the hosted endpoint, a page whose `<footer>` element does not state the manifest's provenance sentence, a ledger page whose source panel does not name the manifest's endpoint, ledger and read time, a commit that is not on the public `main`, a missing or failed verdict, a commit mismatch, any file changed since the gate passed, a ledger page naming another ledger than the manifest, and any failure of the gate's checks run again on the bytes about to be uploaded. It prints the deploy command.
6. **Deploy.** The owner runs the printed `vercel deploy --prebuilt --prod` with the project-scoped token present for that command only. Nothing in this repository runs it.
7. **Compare what is served.** `bun run web/hosted/compare-deployed.ts <dir> https://<name>.vercel.app` fetches every file, and every page at both spellings of its route, and compares status, bytes, content type, the charset as the Encoding Standard resolves it, and security headers with what the gate passed. It also requires a 404 for an unknown path under the same headers, and no cookie.

## The owner's one-time setup, before step 6

From `ADR-0004` D-H4 and "Abuse, availability and cost":

- A Vercel project with **no Git integration**. The Vercel GitHub app would hold write access to the public repository.
- The **Vercel toolbar off** for production and preview. The policy's `script-src` would refuse a script from another origin, and step 7 sees any change to a page's bytes; neither has been tried against the real toolbar.
- A **token scoped to the one project, with an expiry**, kept beside the publication export folder and absent from every build.
- The project's own `vercel.app` name, or a domain used for nothing else. That name is the `URL` the build takes, because the footer names it.
- The **plan, its limits, a spend cap** if the plan offers one, and **who acts when the page goes down**. The ADR asks for these to be named. For the 2026-10-20 deployment the plan is Vercel Hobby, which has no billing and so no spend to cap; the owner acts when it goes down (`CLAIMS.md` E-336, E-337).

## What these checks do not establish

The gate prints its own list. The short form:

- It reads files, so it cannot check egress. The browser check observes one browser on one run.
- The build's first container has network while it installs and takes the snapshot, and on OrbStack it can reach services on the build machine's own loopback through `host.docker.internal` (measured 2026-10-20, `REVIEW-059` S-2). Third-party code that runs during install or the snapshot has that access. The second container, which runs Vite and the assembler, has no network.
- The tree check runs before the containers. It does not see dependency code changing the tree during the build.
- The browser check's visibility test does not see text coloured like its background.
- The gate's secret sweep reads the output without `gate-verdict.json`, the file the gate writes after it. Run on its own over a gated output, `check-no-secrets --dir` can report a verdict digest as a labelled scalar when an asset's hashed name contains `Sk` (`REVIEW-059` R3-12).
- The comparison in step 7 detects a change at the moment it runs, nothing later.
- Every value is a dated reading from Ripple's public Devnet endpoint, which is a pool of servers (`CLAIMS.md` E-327), under the accepted D-H2 risk. Every answer must name the one pinned, validated ledger. The reads are separate requests and are not held to one connection, so a server without that ledger fails the build rather than answering for another.

   197	## Keys
   198	
   199	~~Never write ElGamal keygen code before the Cryptography Reviewer has ruled on ADR-0001 §(e).~~ **Ruled 2026-08-18, implemented and executed 2026-08-20.** The construction lives in `docs/reviews/REVIEW-012-RULING-keygen-2026-08-18.md` and **only** there — do not paraphrase it into the ADR or the architecture document. `signer/src/keygen.rs` is an *implementation* of that prose and says so; where the two disagree, the ruling wins. Derivation from the XRPL master seed is upheld, with five conditions.
   200	
   201	**Three things about that ruling that a coding session will otherwise get wrong:**
   202	
   203	- **Its reduction is TOTAL and has no abort path**, unlike the sigma-nonce and `r`/`ρ` rulings, which both say *abort on zero, never retry*. That is correct there, where the input is fresh entropy and an abort costs one transaction. Keygen's input is a **fixed seed**, so an abort would be permanent — that holder could never participate. Do not "fix" it back.
   204	- **Condition 2 is not cosmetic.** Onboarding must disclose that the master seed becomes a permanent decryption credential, and the signer must **warn** when the account carries `lsfDisableMaster`, a regular key or a signer list (E-69). For those holders the master seed is not spending authority, and §(e)'s argument for derivation does not hold.
   205	- **The registration PoK must always be passed a non-NULL `context_id`.** The library accepts NULL and then emits an account-unbound, replayable proof (F-19). rippled cannot reach that path; our signer can.
   206	
   207	~~When keygen is written, validate it against~~ **Done 2026-08-20.** mpt-crypto's own tests — `test_pok_sk`, `test_elgamal`, `test_elgamal_verify` — are C programs over **random** keys, so `signer/tests/keygen_interop.rs` drives their *properties* with a derived key. **Necessary, not sufficient:** a constant `sk_A = 1` passes all three, and **that claim is now itself a test** — `a_constant_sk_of_one_passes_every_upstream_check` runs it and asserts they pass, which is what makes the KAT load-bearing rather than decorative. The derivation's own KAT is `tools/spike-keygen-2026-08-18/t-keygen-construction.ts` and `signer/tests/keygen_kat.rs`.
   208	
   209	## Testing
   210	
   211	Every capability claim needs a test that fails if the claim is false.
   212	
   213	For confidentiality specifically, **a demo of the happy path is not evidence of confidentiality.** Two harnesses are required:
   214	
   215	- **Observer harness** — shows exactly what a full-ledger observer sees. Its first test is finding F-1 in `CLAIMS.md`: recompute the canonical `EncZero` independently and check whether freshly-initialised and post-merge balances are publicly identifiable as zero.
   216	- **Issuer-view tool** — a single CLI command (`tools/issuer-decrypt-balance.ts`) that reads one account's `sfIssuerEncryptedBalance` and decrypts it given the issuer key. Not a standalone application. Showing what the issuer sees is the honest counterpart to showing what the public sees.

# Visible text of the seven pages of the review build (commit a87ae55), as a browser renders them

Extracted from the built HTML by stripping tags. Diagram boxes appear as short lines; `[caption]` is a diagram's caption, `[nav]` the navigation, and ` | ` separates table cells. The screenshots in `screens/` show the layout.


---

# PAGE: Overview, /

Devnet snapshot · signs nothing, holds no keys · this project is not secure, not audited, not production-ready (P-5)

# W-CONF
What confidential Multi-Purpose Tokens (MPTs) on the XRP Ledger hide, and what they do not. A research project, shown on one snapshot of the Devnet.
[nav] Overview | How it works | What anyone sees | What the issuer sees | Who can do what | Wallets | Custody

## What this site shows
XLS-96, Confidential Transfers, lets a holder of a Multi-Purpose Token (MPT) keep their balance encrypted on the public XRP Ledger: hidden from the public, not from the token’s issuer. This site shows what that hides and what it does not, from one snapshot of the Devnet test network.

A public balance
an ordinary MPT holding
→ ↓ Convert: amount public

Confidential balances

- each holder’s balance, unless it is a recognisable zero: a new balance, an inbox just merged, or a position clawed back
- the amount of each Send between holders
→ ↓ ConvertBack: amount public

A public balance
an ordinary MPT holding

Public everywhere
Who sends to whom, when and how often; the total held in confidential form.

The issuer reads every confidential balance
So does an auditor, if the token names one. No setting turns this off.
public hidden from the public the issuer can read it
[caption] Amounts are public where they cross into or out of confidential form, and hidden inside it. Who transacts with whom is public everywhere, and the issuer reads every balance.
New to this? Read how it works

## Where to go
- How it works: confidential transfers in plain words, and the example this project built.
- What anyone sees: what a ledger observer reads of four test accounts, including which balances are visibly zero.
- What the issuer sees: the encrypted copy of each balance that the issuer can always read. This site does not publish the figures.
- Who can do what: what each kind of key can and cannot do.
- Wallets: why the browser wallets examined cannot hold a confidential position.
- Custody: where confidentiality and custody approval policies conflict.

## About this site
Every value comes from one snapshot of the ledger, taken when this site was built. The site signs nothing and holds no keys. No code of this project runs on a server when you load it, and your browser requests only this site’s own files.

Devnet, a public node
read once, when the site is built
→ ↓ one snapshot

The build
pages built offline, checked by the release gate
→ ↓ static files

Vercel
serves the files; runs none of this project's code
→ ↓ your page load

Your browser
requests only this site's own files

Not part of this site: wconf-signer, on the holder's own machine
Holds the keys, builds the proofs and signs. A web page cannot reach it.
[caption] This site is files built once from a Devnet snapshot. Nothing on it can sign; signing happens only in a separate program on a holder's own machine.
To transact, run it yourself. Signing happens only in wconf-signer , a separate program on the holder’s own machine, never in a web page. The README’s “Run it” explains how to build it and run the local version of this site. It does not yet explain how to create a holder profile and funded Devnet accounts, which a transaction needs.

## What it never claims
- No key source hides identities. The sender and receiver of every confidential transaction are public. (P-4)
- A holder is never private from the issuer. The issuer can always decrypt. (P-9)
- No key source supports rotation or recovery. XLS-96 supports neither. (P-10)
- No key source hides who transacts, when, or how often. Only amounts are hidden. (P-11)
- A newly created confidential balance is publicly identifiable as exactly zero. (F-1)
- Retiring a position in tranches publishes each tranche size. (F-24)
- None of this is secure, audited, non-custodial or production-ready, and the custody mode is a simulation. (P-5)
Each code in brackets names a row in the project’s claims register, CLAIMS.md.

# PAGE: How it works, /how-it-works

Devnet snapshot · signs nothing, holds no keys · this project is not secure, not audited, not production-ready (P-5)

# W-CONF
What confidential Multi-Purpose Tokens (MPTs) on the XRP Ledger hide, and what they do not. A research project, shown on one snapshot of the Devnet.
[nav] Overview | How it works | What anyone sees | What the issuer sees | Who can do what | Wallets | Custody

## Confidential balances, in brief

The XRP Ledger is public: anyone can read every account and every transaction on it. Multi-Purpose Tokens (MPTs, XLS-33) are one of its kinds of token. Confidential Transfers (XLS-96) let an MPT holder keep their balance encrypted on that public ledger: hidden from the public, not from the token’s issuer.
Each confidential balance is stored as an ElGamal ciphertext: a number only the right key can read. A holder registers their own encryption key when they opt in.
The ledger must still check that nobody spends more than they hold. So every transaction that spends from a confidential balance carries zero-knowledge proofs, which every validator checks without learning any hidden amount.
XLS-96 is a final standard. When this page was written, on 2026-10-21, its amendment, ConfidentialTransfer, was enabled on Devnet and not on Mainnet or Testnet.
Sources: E-1, E-349; the README’s terms; TASK ZERO §3

## The life of a confidential position

Holder A

Public balance
↓ Convert: amount public

InboxConfidentialBalanceInbox
↓ MergeInbox

Spending balanceConfidentialBalanceSpending

→ ↓ Send: amount hidden; A and B public

The Send carries
the amount encrypted for the sender, the receiver, the issuer and any auditor, and proofs every validator checks

120:| E-69 | **An XRPL account's master seed need not be its spending authority, which is the fact that falsifies ADR-0001 §(e)'s central defence.** An account may authorise a regular key and **disable the master key**, or enforce multi-signing through a `SignerList`. In those configurations the master seed signs nothing — yet under the keygen ruling it still derives `sk_A` and therefore still decrypts the holder's entire confidential history, permanently, with no rotation. §(e) argues the marginal harm of seed compromise is small because *"seed compromise already means total loss of spending authority"*; **for these holders that premise is false and the confidentiality loss is the whole loss.** This is why the ruling's condition 2 obliges an onboarding disclosure and a signer warning | **Read at source 2026-08-18**, rippled tag `3.3.0` (`00a178f`): `asfDisableMaster` at `include/xrpl/protocol/TxFlags.h:421`; `lsfDisableMaster = 0x00100000`, commented *"True, force regular key"*, at `include/xrpl/protocol/LedgerFormats.h:142`; enforced at `src/libxrpl/tx/Transactor.cpp:1024`; `SetRegularKey.cpp` and `SignerListSet.cpp` present under `src/libxrpl/tx/transactors/account/`. ⚠ **Read, not executed** — no account was configured this way on Devnet and no Convert was attempted from one.     return { what: `public payment to ${to.label}`, hash, amountIsPublic: true, amount };
  }

  /**
   * Move a received amount from the inbox into the spendable balance.
   *
   * ⚠ **Not a signer operation**, and that is not an omission: a
   * `ConfidentialMPTMergeInbox` carries no proof and no blinding factor, so
   * there is nothing for the signer to generate. It is signed here with the
   * party's own seed, like any ordinary XRPL transaction.
   */
  async mergeInbox(p: Party): Promise<Movement> {
    const { hash } = await this.plaintext(
      { TransactionType: "ConfidentialMPTMergeInbox", MPTokenIssuanceID: this.cfg.issuanceId },
      p.seed,
      `${p.label} MergeInbox`,
    );
    // ⚠ The amount is what the inbox held. Recording 0 would put a movement in
    // the log that says nothing moved, on the one transaction whose whole job
    // is to move something.
    const merged = this.book.expected(p.address).inbox;
    this.book.record({ kind: "merge", account: p.address, amount: merged, hash });
    return { what: `${p.label} merged inbox`, hash, amountIsPublic: false, amount: merged };
  }

   143	requires the key when initialising and forbids it afterwards. A supplier with no
   144	registered key **cannot receive a confidential amount at all** — there is no key
   145	to encrypt to, so the transaction cannot be built, let alone rejected.
   146	
   147	### Running the facility
   148	
   149	**Step 6 — drawdown.** Dedicated Account → supplier, one `ConfidentialMPTSend`.
   150	The amount is hidden. Received value lands in the supplier's **inbox**; a
   151	`ConfidentialMPTMergeInbox` moves it into their spendable balance.
   152	
   153	**Step 7 — repayment.** Supplier → Dedicated Account, the same transaction type,
   154	amount hidden.
   155	
   156	**Step 8 — several funders?** They are confidential holders in the same
   157	issuance. The bank sends each their position confidentially, and yield as
   158	further confidential transfers. **No shares and no share price** — positions are
   159	denominated directly, which is a large part of why a `Vault` buys nothing here
   160	(§7).
   161	
   162	### Wind-down
   163	
   164	**Step 9 — `ConfidentialMPTConvertBack`** when the facility retires or resizes.
   165	
   109	
   110	The signer answers three requests. `Pair` opens a grant: a named set of operations, valid for a limited time and only on that socket connection. `Prepare` asks for one transaction under a grant; the signer builds it, shows it to the holder and returns the signed bytes. `Status` carries no authority.
   111	
   112	- **[`signer/`](signer/)** — a Rust binary on the holder's machine. Each instance holds one participant's `sk_A` and XRPL key; in the demonstration the facility writes each party's profile and starts one signer per party. It builds the proofs and signs, and shows what it is about to sign in its own window, parsed from the transaction it built, so a caller that lies in its request cannot get a different transaction confirmed. It refuses every network but Devnet. It reads the ledger and **never submits**, so whatever submits must set `fail_hard`: a failing confidential transaction applied as a `tec` lands on the ledger with its ciphertexts (F-23, E-149). The flag stops that at the ledger, not at the node the blob was handed to (F-28), and nothing forces a submitter to set it (B-15).
   113	- **[`facility/`](facility/)** — the receivables facility: a Dedicated Account, supplier enrolment, drawdowns, repayments, an off-ledger balance book and retirement in tranches. It is a single-machine test driver that plays the bank, the Dedicated Account and every supplier, so it holds every seed and derives every party's `sk_A` from it. It sends the signer an operation, an issuance, a destination and an amount. The signer refuses a request that carries a seed, key or randomness field; that is a check over a named set of field names, not of what a value contains.
   114	- **[`web/`](web/)** — a **read-only** viewer: `web/api` (Elysia, bound to loopback) and `web/app` (TanStack Start, React 19). It renders public ledger state, the observer's view, and an issuer page drawn from a snapshot that `tools/issuer-view-snapshot.ts` wrote; that page says whether the issuer's decryption succeeded, never the figure (E-241). It asks the signer for `Status` only, which [`tools/check-web-readonly.ts`](tools/check-web-readonly.ts) checks by reading `web/api`'s source; it cannot prove what is sent at run time.
   115	- **[`tools/`](tools/)** — the observer harness; the issuer-view tools, which hold the issuer key (`issuer-view-snapshot.ts` writes the viewer's snapshot, `issuer-decrypt-balance.ts` prints a figure); the live amendment reader; and the repository's checks.
   116	
   117	**The signer is a real boundary for the browser, and only for the browser.** No API available to a web page can open a Unix socket, so a script injected into the page cannot open the signer's socket; the most it can reach is `Status`, through `web/api`. That closes the path by construction rather than by an origin check, which any local process can forge. It does not stop an adversary with code execution on the holder's machine. The authority on the design is [`ARCHITECTURE-2026-08-18.md`](docs/architecture/ARCHITECTURE-2026-08-18.md).
   118

# REVIEW-061 — Leg A: register and repository review

## STEP 0 — results

```text
$USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo

No root or scoped `AGENTS.md`/`AGENTS.override.md` was found; the root `CLAUDE.md` was also checked, including its key-source conditions. All review artifacts are under `review-work/`.

## Findings — most severe first

### 1. HIGH — Restrict the two-key claim to operations that require a holder proof

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:293–294`, on `/wallet` and `/capabilities`. **Exact labels:** “A confidential transaction needs both keys. The wallets examined supply only the account key, so a page cannot assemble one through them.” and “A confidential transaction needs both keys: the proof comes from the ElGamal key and the signature from the account key.” **Checked against:** `docs/task-zero/TASK-ZERO-2026-08-13.md:90–97`, CLAIMS E-33 and F-27, and `facility/src/facility.ts:283–302`. `ConfidentialMPTMergeInbox` is a confidential transaction but has no proof and requires no ElGamal secret: the existing facility builds and signs it directly with the account seed, outside the signer. Later Converts also do not require a new registration proof. Thus the captions claim more than the demonstrated two-secret requirement for Send/ConvertBack. The matrix'"'s similar wording does not overcome these counterexamples. **Smallest fix:** say “A holder's confidential Send or ConvertBack needs both keys”; scope the wallet conclusion to assembling those proof-bearing transactions.

### 2. HIGH — Keep the master-key authorisation condition in the key diagram

**Location:** "'`web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:298–302`. **Exact label:** “signs every transaction, public or confidential”, beneath “The account key”, reached from “The master seed” by “XRPL'"'s own derivation gives the account key”. **Checked against:** CLAIMS E-69; "'`web/api/capability.ts:119–131`, the existing-seed row displayed on the same capabilities page; and the explicit instruction in [CLAUDE.md:204](../CLAUDE.md#L204). A master-derived account key need not authorise this account: with `lsfDisableMaster` the signer refuses it; regular-key and signer-list configurations also change the authority analysis. The diagram removes the condition that the matrix deliberately calls “conditional”, while the seed can still derive the permanent viewing key. **Smallest fix:** qualify the master-derived signing branch with “only while the master key authorises the account”; identify an authorised account signing key separately if showing the other regimes.

### 3. MEDIUM — Connect submission to the driver, not the signer

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:250–254`. **Exact edge label:** “the driver submits”. **Checked against:** README “How it fits together”, especially `README.md:110–117`; CLAIMS E-146 and B-15; and the diagram'"'s own sentence “the signer never submits”. The arrow actually leaves the signer box and enters Devnet. At both widths this presents a signer-to-ledger submission path, precisely the boundary the README separates from the driver. Naming the driver on the edge does not repair its endpoints. **Smallest fix:** show signed bytes returning from signer to driver and a distinct driver-to-Devnet submission edge. Keep the API/signer ledger-read paths separate.

### 4. MEDIUM — Reverse the repayment direction

**Location:** "'`web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:214`. **Exact edge label:** “drawdowns and repayments are Sends: amounts hidden; who and when public”. **Checked against:** `docs/explainer/FACILITY-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-20.md:149–154`, steps 6 and 7, and CLAIMS E-146. The sole down arrow runs from the Dedicated Account to suppliers for both operations. That is a drawdown, but a repayment runs supplier → Dedicated Account. Both desktop and phone screenshots retain the wrong one-way grouping. **Smallest fix:** make this connection two-way, with “drawdown” on the outward direction and “repayment” on the return direction.

### 5. MEDIUM — Retire from the Dedicated Account rather than the suppliers

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:220–222`. **Exact edge label:** “retired in tranches: each ConvertBack'"'s amount public”. **Checked against:** "'`facility/src/facility.ts:509–540`, particularly `retire()` selecting `this.cfg.dedicated`, the walkthrough'"'s wind-down, and CLAIMS E-146/B-18/F-24. The retirement arrow follows the three supplier boxes and leads directly to “Back to public balances”. It depicts suppliers cashing out their positions as the facility's retirement, whereas the executed retirement converts the Dedicated Account's remaining float. The public-per-tranche wording itself is correct. **Smallest fix:** connect the retirement/ConvertBack edge to the Dedicated Account, not to the supplier group.

### 6. MEDIUM — Do not colour the public proof as hidden

**Location:** "'`web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:335–338`. **Exact label:** “A proof, not an amount”, with the green key “hidden from the public”. **Checked against:** `web/app/src/views/CustodyView.tsx:48–59`, TASK ZERO'"'s transaction/proof table, and the explainer's statement that every validator checks the proofs. The simulation's policy engine holds only public data and can read the proof; it cannot read the amount. The green box currently assigns secrecy to the proof itself, reversing the distinction the custody finding teaches. **Smallest fix:** make the proof box public or neutral; reserve green for the unreadable amount, if that is drawn separately. The "'$1M policy threshold is a hypothetical rule, not a ledger figure.

### 7. MEDIUM — Distinguish the auditor'"'s ciphertext from the issuer's ciphertext

**Location:** "'`web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:280`, repeated on `/observer` and `/issuer`. **Exact label:** “The auditor'"'s copy, if the token names an auditor”, field "'`AuditorEncryptedBalance`, coloured violet under “the issuer can read it”. **Checked against:** CLAIMS E-58, which decrypts the auditor mirror with the auditor key and the issuer mirror with the issuer key, and the diagram'"'s adjacent field/key labels. At this explicitly field-by-field level the colour says the issuer can read the auditor-encrypted copy. The issuer knows the same balance through its own mandatory mirror; that does not give its key access to the auditor's ciphertext. **Smallest fix:** give the auditor copy a distinct labelled tone, or make it neutral with its existing “the auditor can read it” text. Do not imply that either party's key reads both ciphertexts.

### 8. MEDIUM — Align the lifecycle Send arrow with its source and destination

**Location:** "'`web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:136–153`, particularly the independent centre column at `145–149`. **Exact edge label:** “Send: amount hidden; A and B public”. **Checked against:** CLAIMS F-27/E-33, the lifecycle paragraph on the same page, and `review-input/screens/desk_how-it-works-00.jpg`. At 1100px the Send arrow is near A'"'s public balance and B's inbox, while A's spending balance is much lower. It reads as a public-balance-to-inbox Send, not spending-to-inbox. At 390px the linear ordering does correctly follow A's spending balance. **Smallest fix:** use explicit grid rows/areas for the two connected balances and their Send edge, instead of centring an independent arrow column between unequal vertical stacks.

### 9. MEDIUM — Select an executed script rather than any common JS reference

**Location:** "'`web/hosted/browser-check.ts:341–344`. **Exact selection:** `common[0]`, under the claim “A script chunk every page loads, read from the pages rather than named.” **Checked against:** the supplied HTML, its actual script/import graph, the six changed script-injection controls, and a mutated output copy. The regex includes `modulepreload` links, not just executed scripts. In `review-work/unused-preload/`, adding an unused `/assets/A-unused.js` modulepreload to all seven pages makes the exact helper select that file; `runChecks()` still returns `[]`. Preloading does not evaluate the module, so appending the six control payloads there exercises none of them. This is a false control failure on an otherwise admissible output, not a demonstrated silent PASS. **Smallest fix:** select the actual common module entry script (`script[type=module][src]` here), or establish and verify execution on each required target before using a candidate. Reproducer and output: `review-work/probe-shared.ts` and `review-work/probe-shared.log`.

### 10. LOW — Make the off-screen table column discoverable on a phone

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:182–183`. **Exact heading affected:** “A Send'"'s amount”. **Checked against:** "'`review-input/screens/phone_how-it-works-04.jpg` and the complete desktop table in `desk_how-it-works-01.jpg`. At 390px the final column and all its public/holder/issuer/auditor answers are beyond the visible right edge. There is no visible scroll cue in the screenshot, so the diagram appears to stop at “A holder'"'s balance”. The data is present in the markup, not deleted, but an important disclosure comparison is undiscoverable in the initial phone view. **Smallest fix:** add an explicit horizontal-scroll cue or use a stacked phone presentation that exposes every column.

# REVIEW-061 — Leg A: register and repository review

## STEP 0 — results

## Findings — most severe first

### 1. HIGH — Restrict the two-key claim to operations that require a holder proof

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:293–294`, on `/wallet` and `/capabilities`. **Exact labels:** “A confidential transaction needs both keys. The wallets examined supply only the account key, so a page cannot assemble one through them.” and “A confidential transaction needs both keys: the proof comes from the ElGamal key and the signature from the account key.” **Checked against:** `docs/task-zero/TASK-ZERO-2026-08-13.md:90–97`, CLAIMS E-33 and F-27, and `facility/src/facility.ts:283–302`. `ConfidentialMPTMergeInbox` is a confidential transaction but has no proof and requires no ElGamal secret: the existing facility builds and signs it directly with the account seed, outside the signer. Later Converts also do not require a new registration proof. Thus the captions claim more than the demonstrated two-secret requirement for Send/ConvertBack. The matrix's similar wording does not overcome these counterexamples. **Smallest fix:** say “A holder's confidential Send or ConvertBack needs both keys”; scope the wallet conclusion to assembling those proof-bearing transactions.

### 2. HIGH — Keep the master-key authorisation condition in the key diagram

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:298–302`. **Exact label:** “signs every transaction, public or confidential”, beneath “The account key”, reached from “The master seed” by “XRPL's own derivation gives the account key”. **Checked against:** CLAIMS E-69; `web/api/capability.ts:119–131`, the existing-seed row displayed on the same capabilities page; and the explicit instruction in [CLAUDE.md:204](../CLAUDE.md#L204). A master-derived account key need not authorise this account: with `lsfDisableMaster` the signer refuses it; regular-key and signer-list configurations also change the authority analysis. The diagram removes the condition that the matrix deliberately calls “conditional”, while the seed can still derive the permanent viewing key. **Smallest fix:** qualify the master-derived signing branch with “only while the master key authorises the account”; identify an authorised account signing key separately if showing the other regimes.

### 3. MEDIUM — Connect submission to the driver, not the signer

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:250–254`. **Exact edge label:** “the driver submits”. **Checked against:** README “How it fits together”, especially `README.md:110–117`; CLAIMS E-146 and B-15; and the diagram's own sentence “the signer never submits”. The arrow actually leaves the signer box and enters Devnet. At both widths this presents a signer-to-ledger submission path, precisely the boundary the README separates from the driver. Naming the driver on the edge does not repair its endpoints. **Smallest fix:** show signed bytes returning from signer to driver and a distinct driver-to-Devnet submission edge. Keep the API/signer ledger-read paths separate.

### 4. MEDIUM — Reverse the repayment direction

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:214`. **Exact edge label:** “drawdowns and repayments are Sends: amounts hidden; who and when public”. **Checked against:** `docs/explainer/FACILITY-WALKTHROUGH-2026-08-20.md:149–154`, steps 6 and 7, and CLAIMS E-146. The sole down arrow runs from the Dedicated Account to suppliers for both operations. That is a drawdown, but a repayment runs supplier → Dedicated Account. Both desktop and phone screenshots retain the wrong one-way grouping. **Smallest fix:** make this connection two-way, with “drawdown” on the outward direction and “repayment” on the return direction.

### 5. MEDIUM — Retire from the Dedicated Account rather than the suppliers

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:220–222`. **Exact edge label:** “retired in tranches: each ConvertBack's amount public”. **Checked against:** `facility/src/facility.ts:509–540`, particularly `retire()` selecting `this.cfg.dedicated`, the walkthrough's wind-down, and CLAIMS E-146/B-18/F-24. The retirement arrow follows the three supplier boxes and leads directly to “Back to public balances”. It depicts suppliers cashing out their positions as the facility's retirement, whereas the executed retirement converts the Dedicated Account's remaining float. The public-per-tranche wording itself is correct. **Smallest fix:** connect the retirement/ConvertBack edge to the Dedicated Account, not to the supplier group.

### 6. MEDIUM — Do not colour the public proof as hidden

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:335–338`. **Exact label:** “A proof, not an amount”, with the green key “hidden from the public”. **Checked against:** `web/app/src/views/CustodyView.tsx:48–59`, TASK ZERO's transaction/proof table, and the explainer's statement that every validator checks the proofs. The simulation's policy engine holds only public data and can read the proof; it cannot read the amount. The green box currently assigns secrecy to the proof itself, reversing the distinction the custody finding teaches. **Smallest fix:** make the proof box public or neutral; reserve green for the unreadable amount, if that is drawn separately. The $1M policy threshold is a hypothetical rule, not a ledger figure.

### 7. MEDIUM — Distinguish the auditor's ciphertext from the issuer's ciphertext

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:280`, repeated on `/observer` and `/issuer`. **Exact label:** “The auditor's copy, if the token names an auditor”, field `AuditorEncryptedBalance`, coloured violet under “the issuer can read it”. **Checked against:** CLAIMS E-58, which decrypts the auditor mirror with the auditor key and the issuer mirror with the issuer key, and the diagram's adjacent field/key labels. At this explicitly field-by-field level the colour says the issuer can read the auditor-encrypted copy. The issuer knows the same balance through its own mandatory mirror; that does not give its key access to the auditor's ciphertext. **Smallest fix:** give the auditor copy a distinct labelled tone, or make it neutral with its existing “the auditor can read it” text. Do not imply that either party's key reads both ciphertexts.

### 8. MEDIUM — Align the lifecycle Send arrow with its source and destination

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:136–153`, particularly the independent centre column at `145–149`. **Exact edge label:** “Send: amount hidden; A and B public”. **Checked against:** CLAIMS F-27/E-33, the lifecycle paragraph on the same page, and `review-input/screens/desk_how-it-works-00.jpg`. At 1100px the Send arrow is near A's public balance and B's inbox, while A's spending balance is much lower. It reads as a public-balance-to-inbox Send, not spending-to-inbox. At 390px the linear ordering does correctly follow A's spending balance. **Smallest fix:** use explicit grid rows/areas for the two connected balances and their Send edge, instead of centring an independent arrow column between unequal vertical stacks.

### 9. MEDIUM — Select an executed script rather than any common JS reference

**Location:** `web/hosted/browser-check.ts:341–344`. **Exact selection:** `common[0]`, under the claim “A script chunk every page loads, read from the pages rather than named.” **Checked against:** the supplied HTML, its actual script/import graph, the six changed script-injection controls, and a mutated output copy. The regex includes `modulepreload` links, not just executed scripts. In `review-work/unused-preload/`, adding an unused `/assets/A-unused.js` modulepreload to all seven pages makes the exact helper select that file; `runChecks()` still returns `[]`. Preloading does not evaluate the module, so appending the six control payloads there exercises none of them. This is a false control failure on an otherwise admissible output, not a demonstrated silent PASS. **Smallest fix:** select the actual common module entry script (`script[type=module][src]` here), or establish and verify execution on each required target before using a candidate. Reproducer and output: `review-work/probe-shared.ts` and `review-work/probe-shared.log`.

### 10. LOW — Make the off-screen table column discoverable on a phone

**Location:** `web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:182–183`. **Exact heading affected:** “A Send's amount”. **Checked against:** `review-input/screens/phone_how-it-works-04.jpg` and the complete desktop table in `desk_how-it-works-01.jpg`. At 390px the final column and all its public/holder/issuer/auditor answers are beyond the visible right edge. There is no visible scroll cue in the screenshot, so the diagram appears to stop at “A holder's balance”. The data is present in the markup, not deleted, but an important disclosure comparison is undiscoverable in the initial phone view. **Smallest fix:** add an explicit horizontal-scroll cue or use a stacked phone presentation that exposes every column.

The diagrams introduce overbroad key claims and misleading transaction paths and colours. The full report, evidence, test results and browser-execution limitations were written to review-work/report.md and printed to stdout.

Full review comments:

- [P1] Scope the two-key requirement to proof-bearing holder operations — $USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo/web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:293-294
  `ConfidentialMPTMergeInbox` needs no ElGamal secret or proof: `facility/src/facility.ts:283–302` builds and signs it directly with the account seed. TASK ZERO §3 likewise identifies it as proof-free, while E-33 establishes the holder-secret requirement specifically for Send and ConvertBack. Both new captions therefore overstate the requirement. Scope them to a holder's Send or ConvertBack rather than every confidential transaction.

- [P1] Preserve the master-key authorisation condition — $USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo/web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:302-302
  When `lsfDisableMaster` is set, the master-derived account key shown by this branch cannot authorise transactions, although the seed still derives the viewing key. E-69 and the capabilities page's existing-seed row explicitly retain this distinction; [CLAUDE.md:204](CLAUDE.md#L204) also requires it. Qualify this label with the condition that the master key still authorises the account.

- [P2] Draw submission from the driver rather than the signer — $USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo/web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:253-254
  At both screenshot widths, this arrow leaves `wconf-signer` and enters Devnet, despite saying “the driver submits”. README's “How it fits together” and B-15 deliberately separate the signer, which returns signed bytes and never submits, from the submitting driver. Show the signed-byte return to the driver and connect the submission edge from the driver to Devnet.

- [P2] Give repayments their return direction — $USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo/web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:214-214
  This down arrow represents both operations as Dedicated Account → supplier. The walkthrough's steps 6–7 explicitly distinguish drawdowns in that direction from repayments running supplier → Dedicated Account. Make the connection bidirectional or draw separately labelled outward and return edges.

- [P2] Connect retirement to the Dedicated Account — $USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo/web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:221-222
  Placed after the supplier group, this arrow depicts suppliers converting their positions back as the facility's retirement. In the executed facility, `retire()` explicitly performs ConvertBack for `this.cfg.dedicated` (`facility/src/facility.ts:509–540`), matching E-146/B-18. Keep the accurate public-per-tranche label, but originate its edge at the Dedicated Account.

- [P2] Colour the proof as public rather than hidden — $USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo/web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:335-335
  The green legend means “hidden from the public”, but this box names the proof, which the public-only approval workflow can read and validators check. `CustodyView.tsx` distinguishes that public proof from the unreadable amount; the diagram's colour reverses that distinction. Make the proof box public or neutral and reserve green for the hidden amount.

- [P2] Distinguish auditor-readable and issuer-readable ciphertexts — $USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo/web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:280-280
  On both position pages, violet means “the issuer can read it”, but this box specifically identifies `AuditorEncryptedBalance`. E-58 uses the auditor key for that ciphertext and the issuer key for the separate issuer mirror. Knowing the same balance through its own mirror does not let the issuer read the auditor ciphertext. Use a distinct auditor-labelled tone or a neutral box.

- [P2] Align the desktop Send edge with the spending balance — $USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo/web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:145-149
  In `desk_how-it-works-00.jpg`, the independently centred Send arrow sits beside A's public balance and B's inbox, well above A's spending balance. It consequently reads as public-balance → inbox, contrary to F-27 and the page's spending-to-inbox explanation. Use explicit grid areas for the connected fields and edge; the phone's linear ordering already preserves the intended source.

- [P2] Select an executed script instead of any common JS reference — $USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo/web/hosted/browser-check.ts:341-344
  A common `modulepreload` also matches this regex, although preloading does not evaluate its code. On a supplied-build copy, adding an unused `/assets/A-unused.js` preload to every page makes this helper select it while exported `runChecks()` still passes. All six injected controls would then remain unexecuted and falsely fail. Select the common module entry script or verify candidate execution. - [P3] Make the off-screen disclosure column discoverable — $USER_TMP/T/r061a.NchKmT/repo/web/app/src/hosted/Diagrams.tsx:182-183
  At 390px, `phone_how-it-works-04.jpg` hides the entire “A Send's amount” column beyond the right edge, without a visible scrolling cue. The diagram appears to end at holder balances, concealing a central disclosure comparison. Add an explicit horizontal-scroll hint or a stacked phone presentation.
